# Independent chess-learning journal

## Starting state — 2026-10-03

Read this experiment's AGENTS.md and GOAL.md. The referee reports zero started benchmark games, no active game, and no Elo estimate. No imported learning material, outside position advice, or engine analysis has been used. An active goal is registered without a token budget. Actual token counters are unavailable; no usage values will be invented.

The target is stronger play, with the bounded endpoint of 200 scored benchmark games. All moves will be my own decisions. Full-strength results remain part of the benchmark, although the current estimator excludes that uncalibrated opponent.

## Trial 1: position tracking and tactical safety

Hypothesis: maintaining a reliable board model and checking the opponent's forcing replies will reduce avoidable material losses. This is an unverified self-assessment, not a measured diagnosis yet.

Initial study: review ordinary opening priorities from my existing knowledge: contest the center, develop pieces, secure the king, and avoid unnecessary pawn moves. In tactical positions, consider checks, captures, and direct threats for both sides. Before committing a move, mentally update the occupied squares and check whether the move leaves a piece or mating square unprotected. Favor concrete calculation over relying on the checklist alone.

First evaluation point: six scored benchmarks, covering one paired-color rotation. After each game, record one concrete failure or successful behavior and choose a learning adjustment before the next game. Use recorded opponent practice when a specific weakness calls for a drill. Baseline results will be descriptive; six games cannot establish a reliable improvement trend.

Observable evidence: recurrence of pieces lost to an immediate forcing reply; ability to reach playable middlegames without such losses; benchmark results and the referee's Elo interval. An easier practice win alone will not establish transfer.

### Initial repertoire choice

White: begin with 1.e4, develop naturally, and adapt to the reply. Black: meet 1.e4 with ...e5 and 1.d4 with ...d5 unless a concrete position warrants another choice. These are my own familiar starting choices, not externally recommended moves.

## Benchmark 1 — loss, White versus full strength

Resigned after 22...Bxf4, 44 plies, with an extra enemy minor piece and no identified compensation. No illegal submissions. This full-strength game does not supply a calibrated Elo observation.

Concrete failure: after 19...g5, I played 20.Kg2 gxf4 21.Bxf4 Qxf4 22.Qxf4 Bxf4. I failed to count the queen and bishop attacking f4. The queen trade did not recover the bishop. Looking back, my bishop on e3 also had its retreat squares obstructed by my own queen and pawns; this was more than a recapture oversight. The king-safety plan overlooked that trapped-piece consequence.

Earlier concern: 15.Qd2 permitted ...Bxf3 with g-pawn recapture because my bishop on e3 blocked the queen's route to f3. These are my own assessments, without engine validation.

## Trial 2: complete exchanges and preserve an escape square

Learning period: mentally revisit the position after 19...g5. Count material through the entire exchange, not just the first recapture, and check available bishop retreats after pawn captures. The actual continuation loses a bishop; a proposed 20.f5 opens f4 as a retreat square and avoids ...gxf4. Its overall quality is unverified and its king-safety consequences require play.

Recorded practice: one short drill from benchmark 1, ply 38, against skill 5. This is a remembered position, not a new transfer test. Try my own alternative 20.f5, then finish or explicitly end the drill after observing the immediate consequences. Next measured evaluation: benchmark 2, with the six-game rotation still the first aggregate checkpoint.

Observable result: avoid the specific forced bishop loss in the drill; in new benchmark play, resolve pawn attacks on blocked pieces before making unrelated moves. Neither a corrected remembered position nor a practice result establishes stronger general play.

### Trial 2 outcome and decision

Practice 1 was explicitly ended as an unfinished drill after 20.f5 Qh2+ 21.Kf1 Qxh3+ 22.Ke2 Qf3+ 23.Kf1 a4. The bishop remained on e3, but I lost h3 and my king was displaced. Thus the narrow trap was avoided; the alternative was not shown to equalize or win. No practice score is claimed.

Decision for benchmark 2: apply complete exchange counting to new positions and inspect mobility when a pawn advance closes a bishop's diagonals. In opening pins, prefer development and a recapture that preserves king cover. The original first aggregate evaluation remains six scored games. Do not claim transfer until these behaviors survive new benchmark positions.

## Benchmark 2 — loss, Black versus full strength

Checkmated at 33.Nh4#, 65 plies, no illegal submissions. Material remained equal through the opening and the bishop exchange. I did not anticipate 23.Qxa8 after 22...axb4 cleared the a-file: the continuation traded White's queen for both Black rooks. Material comparison alone missed the resulting rook coordination against my king.

A direct error followed: 25...c3 26.Rc1 Qe7 27.Rxc3. Moving the queen removed the pawn's only defender. Later the rooks on a8/c8 overwhelmed Bf8; 29...Kh7 30.Rxf8 was followed by Rh8+, Bh6+, and Nh4#. My own diagnosis is lost-defense and king-flight errors, not merely unfamiliar opening knowledge.

Transfer assessment: the specific trapped-bishop loss from game 1 did not recur, but elementary tactical omissions did. There is no evidence yet that the first method improved benchmark strength. Both completed games are uncalibrated maximum-strength losses, so Elo remains unavailable.

## Trial 3: vacated lines and lost defenders

Learning period before benchmark 3: mentally revisit two remembered positions from game 2. At move 22, the pawn on a5 was masking the rook on a8 from Qa4; ...axb4 removed that mask. At move 26, Qc7 was the only defender of c3; ...Qe7 removed it. These are concrete line changes visible from the recorded moves, without external evaluation. A candidate such as retaining the queen on c7 and creating a king flight square is only an untested alternative.

Changed method: after choosing a candidate, inspect the origin square as well as the destination. Name the specific piece or pawn that loses protection and any line that opens; then consider the opponent's available checks and captures on those lines. Do this concretely, rather than treating completion of a checklist as success.

Next evaluation: benchmark 3 now, and the aggregate checkpoint at six scored games. Observable transfer: no unanticipated immediate capture caused by vacating a blocker or sole defender. Recurrence will count against the method even if a weaker opponent fails to exploit it.

## Benchmark 3 — win, White versus skill 0

Checkmated at 43.Rf8#, 85 plies, no illegal submissions. This is the first calibrated result. Opponent mistakes allowed two knights and then the queen to be won. I converted the remaining rook-and-bishop advantage after a later mistake.

Errors: 9.Qxf3 left d4 without a defender, allowing ...Nxd4. Later 23.Re8+ Rxe8 24.Qxe8+ Kxe8 demonstrated another incomplete exchange calculation: I had not accounted for the final king capture of the queen. The position remained won because of the much larger prior material advantage, not because that combination was sound.

Successful behavior: the final endgame kept the rook safe while the king advanced, captured the remaining pawns, and explicitly avoided stalemate. At move 41, Bh4 released b8 as a legal king move before Kb6 and Rf8#. This was a concrete correct conversion in a favorable position, not proof of general improvement.

Trial 3 assessment: failed its targeted transfer criterion; the lost-defender error recurred. A win against skill 0 does not override that evidence.

## Trial 4: final-position verification in forcing sequences

Learning period: revisit benchmark 3 after 22...Rc8 (ply 44), a remembered position. Check the resulting occupied squares and the defending piece after every capture, including a king recapture. A check is not mate unless every king destination, capture of the checking piece, and available block has been examined.

My own proposed correction is 23.Qxf6+ rather than sacrificing the rook on e8. It preserves the rook as a defender. Its consequences will be tested with a short recorded skill-5 drill, without requesting analysis. Next transfer test is benchmark 4; the first aggregate checkpoint remains six scored games.

Observable result: verify the drill's actual outcome, then avoid a queen or rook loss from a final king recapture in new play. No claim that a memorized drill alone demonstrates learning transfer.

### Trial 4 drill outcome

Practice 2 ended in checkmate after 23.Qxf6+ Kd7 24.Qe7#, three new plies, no illegal attempts. The rook on e6 protected the queen on e7. The referee verified the outcome; no engine analysis or advice was requested. This corrects the remembered example but does not yet show transfer.

After three scored benchmarks, the referee reports Elo 2070 with 95% credible interval 1110–3350, based on one calibrated win and excluding the two full-strength losses. This is a very small sample, conditional on nominal engine anchors and estimator assumptions, not a human rating.

Decision for benchmark 4: retain the correction's principle—explicitly verify the defender remaining in the final position—and watch the origin-square defenses. Next aggregate evaluation remains six scored benchmarks.

## Benchmark 4 — win, Black versus skill 0

Checkmated with 20...Qd1#, 40 plies, no illegal submissions. B g4 protected Q d1 through f3/e2, and I checked that the king could not capture the queen. This is a successful use of the final-defender principle in a new position, but one favorable example is weak transfer evidence.

The opening preserved king cover and supported ...e5 with Re8/Nc6. The ...e4 pawn advance attacked the bishop and knight; White's subsequent captures and failure to recapture the queen allowed a decisive advantage. I do not claim that winning the queen was forced against accurate defense. No obvious own immediate material-loss oversight was identified in this game; that is a self-assessment without independent analysis.

## Trial 5: direct development with explicit center support

Learning period before benchmark 5: contrast my own openings. In game 1 the bishop and remaining knight made several early moves before the c1 bishop developed. In game 4 castling and rook support made a central advance possible. The hypothesis is that simpler development with maintained pawn defenses will reduce restricted-piece and lost-defender situations; this remains unverified.

White experiment: if the reply permits, develop the king's bishop to c4 and the queen's knight to c3, castle, and develop the c1 bishop without neglecting the center. Adapt to actual replies rather than force an opening sequence. Keep the final-position verification from trial 4. This is my own repertoire change from existing knowledge, with no external source.

Next evaluation: games 5–6 against the fixed 1800 setting, followed by the six-game aggregate checkpoint and a new learning decision between games. Observable result: reach a developed middlegame with material intact and no unanticipated lost defender; record any exception separately from W/D/L.

## Benchmark 5 — loss, White versus the 1800 setting

Resigned after 37...Rxg2, 74 plies, no illegal submissions. A full rook was missing, pawn counts were equal, and I identified no compensation. No loss was discarded.

The opening adapted to ...Nc6/...e6: queens were exchanged, both bishops developed, and I castled long. Material remained equal through move 34. This met the narrow development objective, but did not produce a successful game.

Errors and warnings: 23.Kd2 blocked the rear rook and 23...Bb2 exposed the advanced rook on d4. I overlooked its restricted retreat squares and the effect of occupying d2. The opponent allowed ...Ne7 instead of taking the rook; 25.R4d2 saved it. That was not proof that the earlier position was safe. The decisive direct blunder was 35.Rf4: the black pawn on e5 could capture f4. 35...exf4 36.Rxf4 left me down a rook for a pawn, and subsequent ...Rxg2 removed the pawn difference. My longer prospective calculations had failed to check a basic attack in the actual position.

Correct new-position behavior: 20.Bh2 preserved the e5 pawn's defense while avoiding the black pawn on h4, and 27.Rf2 covered g2 before the knight moved off e4. These successes coexist with the later failure; they do not establish improved strength.

Trial 5 assessment: direct development transferred to a materially intact queenless middlegame. Tactical reliability did not; the game was lost to a one-move pawn capture.

## Trial 6: current pawn attacks before destination choice

Learning period before benchmark 6: revisit the decisive remembered position after 34...e5. Mentally mark Black's pawn attacks: a4 -> b3; b7 -> a6/c6; c7 -> b6/d6; e5 -> d4/f4; f6 -> e5/g5; h4 -> g3. The proposed rook destination f4 was directly on this list. Support behind a rook does not make trading it for a pawn acceptable.

Method change: first reconstruct the current pawn locations from the referee board, then check the destination against those attacks before calculating longer variations. Keep the full final-position and lost-defender checks, but use them on the actual board instead of an imagined future continuation. This is a targeted procedural trial, not a claim of learning by checklist length.

Next evaluation: benchmark 6, then the first six-game aggregate checkpoint. Observable result: avoid a direct pawn-controlled destination that loses a more valuable piece; record any occurrence even if the opponent misses it. No practice result or transfer is claimed for this mental review of a remembered example.

## Benchmark 6 — win, Black versus the 1800 setting

Checkmated with 69...Ng3#, 138 plies. One illegal submission: at move 21 I attempted e5f6 for a knight, mistaking a diagonal step for a knight move. The referee rejected it, the position stayed unchanged, and the legal continuation was 21...d4. This geometry failure must not be hidden by the win.

The opening exchanges left an extra pawn and White's bishop pair. I maintained pawn support through ...d4, ...Bc3, ...Bd2, and ...dxe3. The opponent's active rook required repeated knight defenses. ...g6 created king flight before the rook left the back rank. Rook and bishop exchanges eventually produced knight versus bishop with four pawns against three. These are descriptions of the played position, not engine evaluations.

The kingside exchanges, supported ...f4, ...Ng3, ...Nf5, and ...Nxh4 eventually produced two passers against the a-pawn. Conversion was slow and uncertain. I repeatedly reconsidered long hypothetical lines, including imagined drawn fortresses and king routes, without efficiently resolving the actual position. After ...h2 Bxf3+ ...Nxf3, White's king on g2 prevented an immediate safe promotion. 64...Kf4 65.a5 Nd4 66.Kh1 allowed ...Kg3, ...Kh3, and ...Nf5. White had to move its a-pawn because its king had no legal move. The final position after a8=N permitted ...Ng3#: h2 covered g1, Kh3 covered g2 and h2, and the distant promoted knight could not remove the check. I explicitly checked that a queen promotion on a8 would also not capture g3 or resolve that knight check; this is my own calculation, not a separate recorded result.

Trial 6 assessment: no direct pawn-controlled destination causing a rook or minor-piece loss was identified in this game. The targeted check helped in several actual choices, but the illegal knight move and lengthy board drift expose separate reliability problems. One win does not establish consistent transfer.

## First aggregate checkpoint — six scored benchmarks

Referee status: 3 wins, 0 draws, 3 losses. Full strength: 0/0/2 W/D/L; skill 0: 2/0/0; 1800 setting: 1/0/1. All six started benchmarks finished and remain recorded. Two recorded practices: one unfinished drill and one win; neither contributes to these counts.

Provisional engine-relative Elo 1870, 95% Bayesian credible interval 1410–2460. Four calibrated observations; both full-strength games excluded by the operator's estimator. Conditional on nominal anchors and model assumptions, excluding systematic calibration error; not human/FIDE/platform Elo. Earlier estimates of 2070 and 2210 were based only on skill-0 wins; the changes to 1680 and 1870 after the two 1800 games are primarily new evidence, not a demonstrated learning curve. Improvement or stagnation cannot yet be established.

Observed active-goal counter from get_goal: 779650 cumulative tokens at this checkpoint. Recorded through play usage with its scope: all goal learning and play, no per-move attribution. This is an actual tool counter; no inferred or invented token values. The experiment has no token budget.

Decision: retain natural development and final-defender checks, but reduce speculative analysis that loses current geometry. Continue the fixed rotation. Next aggregate evaluation: 12 scored benchmarks; revise the trial after each game.

## Trial 7: current geometry before extended calculation

Learning period: revisit the rejected move in game 6. A knight changes one coordinate by two and the other by one; e5f6 changes both by one and is not a knight move. Mentally reconstruct e5's eight geometric destinations: c4, c6, d3, d7, f3, f7, g4, g6; then distinguish occupancy and opponent pawn attacks from geometry. This is my own mental exercise, not an automated legal-move menu. At the remembered position, f7 held my pawn, g4 was attacked by h3, and f3 by g2.

Also revisit the actual ending: the a-pawn's forced moves supplied time for a knight mate even though it promoted. Counting a promotion race alone was insufficient; the distant promoted piece's ability to answer the immediate check mattered. Keep that as a concrete calculation principle, without treating the memorized mate as general mastery.

Changed method for new play: reconstruct occupied squares and the opponent's immediate threat first. Compare a small number of candidates on the current board, verify their movement geometry and immediate captures, and only extend lines when there is a concrete forcing reason. When a line becomes too long to track confidently, return to the referee's current board. No fixed inference-token cap is imposed; the trial concerns attention and position accuracy.

Next evaluation: benchmark 7, followed by the 12-game aggregate checkpoint. Observable outcomes: illegal submissions, immediate material losses from geometry or pawn attacks, and actual benchmark results. A successful remembered calculation alone will not count as transfer.

## Benchmark 7 — loss, White versus full strength

Resigned after 23...Qg3, 46 plies, no illegal submissions. The threat ...Qxg2# was supported by the rook on f2; I found no satisfactory defence. This is my own calculation of the threat, not a separately played mate. The loss remains recorded and is excluded from the calibrated estimator as a full-strength result.

Natural development, both knights, castling, and the central ...exd4/Bxd4/...Nxd4/Rxd4 exchange reached move 18 with equal material. The final recapture on d4 was correctly supported and did not repeat game 1's incomplete exchange loss.

Decisive failure: 19.Rf1 answered the pressure on f2 but allowed 19...Nxg3, forking Qe2 and Rf1. The g3 pawn appeared defended by f2, but moving f2 exposed the rook to the f-file battery. I played Qd3 ...Nxf1 Qxf1, losing the exchange and a pawn. ...Rxf2 won another pawn. Qb5 sought counterplay against the bishop but was far from the king; ...Qf4, Ng2, and ...Qg3 produced the final mating threat. This was a concrete missed fork and overloaded pawn defender, not a movement-geometry error.

Trial 7 assessment: no illegal moves and no identified minor piece or rook placed directly under a losing pawn capture. The tactical objective still failed. Speculative analysis remained long and repeatedly required rechecking the same current facts. Correct geometry alone does not supply tactical reliability.

## Trial 8: overloaded defenders and knight forks

Learning period: mentally revisit game 7 after 18...Rf8 (ply 36). Nh5 could reach g3, attacking e2 and f1 together. Moving the rook to f1 created that fork. The apparent defender f2 also blocked the stacked f-file attack, so its ability to recapture could not be assumed from pawn geometry alone.

Own proposed trial: 19.Qg4 instead of 19.Rf1, moving the queen off e2 and supporting g3 directly. It may concede f2 or create other threats; it is not claimed to equalize or be best. Test it in a recorded skill-8 practice from that saved position, for up to eight own moves, ending the drill explicitly if it remains active. This is a remembered-position test, not a new transfer benchmark.

New-position method: when relying on a defender, check what its move exposes. Before moving a queen or rook, inspect the opponent knight's reachable squares for attacks on two valuable pieces. Keep the current-board and pawn-destination checks; do not infer success merely from having considered them.

Next evaluations: the short practice's actual outcome, then benchmark 8. Aggregate checkpoint remains 12 scored games. Record whether the fork is avoided, any new losses, and new-position recurrence separately from the benchmark score.

### Trial 8 practice outcome and decision — 2026-10-04

Practice 3 ended in a loss by checkmate after five own moves, ten new plies, no illegal submissions: Qg4 Rxf2 Qg6 Rf1+ Rxf1 Qxf1+ Kh2 Rf2+ Ng2 Qxg2#. The g3 fork of queen and rook did not occur, but the retained pawn cover restricted my king's escape squares and the f-file invasion still won. Qg6's proposed queen exchange did not force a trade. The alternative was not shown to repair the position.

Decision: reject the remembered Qg4 plan as a tested solution. The immediate fork was one symptom of a broader king-safety failure. In benchmark 8, preserve king cover while developing and inspect actual checks before seeking counterplay. When counting defenders, distinguish a possible recapture from one that leaves the king in check. No improvement or transfer is claimed from this failed drill. Return to the fixed benchmark rotation now; aggregate checkpoint remains 12 scored games.

## Benchmark 8 — loss, Black versus full strength

Resigned after 26.Rf6+, 51 plies, no illegal submissions. I was down a full rook for one extra pawn. The supported c-pawn and king activity had not recovered material; I identified no adequate compensation. This loss remains part of the fixed rotation.

Opening trial: 3...Nf6 led to ...Nxe4, ...Nd6, ...dxc6, ...Nf5, and Qxd8+ Kxd8. Queens were exchanged with equal material after White recovered the e5 pawn. ...Be6, ...Bc5, Nxe6+ ...fxe6, and ...Ne7 reached a queenless position with both rooks and one bishop/knight per side. This description does not claim equality of the position.

First failure: 14...Kd7 guarded e6 against Ng5 but invited 15.Rd1+. I blocked with ...Nd5, supported by c6, then 16.c4 attacked that knight. It could not retreat because the rook pinned it to my king. ...Ke7 cxd5 cxd5 lost the knight for the c-pawn. The earlier analysis counted the block's defender but failed to check whether the blocking piece would remain mobile under a pawn attack.

Second failure: after ...h6, Nh3, ...c5, and Nf4, I played 20...g5 to chase the knight. 21.Ng6+ forked Ke7 and Rh8. ...Kf7 Nxh8+ ...Rxh8 lost the exchange as well, leaving a full rook missing. The h7 pawn had ceased guarding g6 after ...h6. I had considered some knight retreats but missed the immediate checking fork. This is another new-position recurrence of the trial's targeted weakness.

Counterplay attempt: ...c4 made a supported passer; ...Kg6 and ...hxg5 responded to f4/fxg5. White's Rf6+ kept my king under pressure. I resigned without claiming that a conversion had been proved impossible.

Trial 8 transfer assessment: failed. The remembered Qg4 drill lost, and a checking knight fork still occurred in the next benchmark. Pawn cover and queen removal did not cure elementary tactical omissions. Eight scored benchmarks now stand at 3 wins, 0 draws, 5 losses: full strength 0/0/4, skill 0 2/0/0, 1800 setting 1/0/1. The next aggregate rating check remains game 12.

## Trial 9: checks and pins before positional plans

Learning period: mentally revisit two actual geometries from game 8. With Rd1, Nd5, and Kd7 on the same file, the knight could not move off that file without exposing check. A pawn defender on c6 did not solve the mobility problem. With White Nf4, Black Ke7/Rh8, and h7 already moved to h6, g6 was an undefended checking-fork square. The knight's eight geometric destinations from f4 are d3, d5, e2, e6, g2, g6, h3, h5; the checking move needed priority over assumptions about a retreat.

Own untested comparison: ...Ng6 instead of ...Kd7 after White's earlier Ng5 would move the knight off e7 and retain h7's defence of g6, while possibly conceding e6. It is not claimed to be best or sufficient. The concrete lesson is to compare a pawn concession with the risk of immobilizing a more valuable piece; do not assume maintaining a pawn's defender makes the whole continuation safe.

New-position method: before a positional pawn or king move, check the opponent's immediate checks, especially knight checks that also attack a rook or queen. For a piece blocking check, inspect the pin and the opponent's pawn attacks on that blocker. Use a compact current-board calculation; if analysis repeats long speculative lines, reset to the actual occupied squares before committing.

Repertoire trial for the next White benchmarks: start with 1.d4 and develop naturally, keeping the king's pawn cover when possible. This changes the type of early positions; it does not claim a particular opening is stronger. Adapt to the replies and keep recording tactical mistakes even against skill 0. Benchmark 9 is the immediate evaluation; benchmark 11 tests the White change against the 1800 setting. Aggregate checkpoint remains 12 scored games. No engine analysis, external advice, or automated chess analysis has been used.

## Benchmark 9 — win, White versus skill 0

Checkmate by 64.Qe8#, 127 plies, one illegal submission. Nine scored benchmarks now total 4 wins, 0 draws, 5 losses: full strength 0/0/4 W/D/L; skill 0 3/0/0; 1800 setting 1/0/1. No separate learning period occurred while this measured game was active.

The 1.d4 trial developed Nf3, c4, Nc3, e3, Bd3, castling, b3/Bb2, and Rac1. After the bishop exchange, e4/e5 gained space. Nxb7 and Qxc6 took two pawns; Qxc8 Qxc8 Rxc8 Rxc8 exchanged queens and one rook each without changing that pawn lead. This is a description of material and play, not a claim that the opening established a superior position.

Black's remaining rook was active on the second rank. I avoided bxa4 while it would remove b3's defence of Nc4, then moved the knight and defended b3 with Nd2. At move 30 I nevertheless attempted c4b3 as a knight move, which is diagonal and illegal. The board was unchanged; the correct d2b3 recapture followed. This repeats game 6's movement-geometry failure despite prior study.

Black later left its rook on a2 under attack: 36.Rxa2 Nxc1 gained the exchange, followed by Nxe6, Rxd5, and Rxa5 as more material became undefended. The decisive own error was 45.Rf6+ gxf6: I checked whether the king could take the rook, but missed the g7 pawn's capture of f6. The rook was lost for no material. The intended forcing line was not forced. This repeats game 5's rook-on-pawn-attack failure, now in a checking move rather than a quiet move.

The remaining advantage survived. exf6 made a passer. Nh7 supported f6 and f8; moving the other knight from f7 to g5 both cleared the pawn and protected f7 and Nh7. After answering ...Nd2+, f8=Q promoted. The opponent knight supplied further checks, but was eventually left beside my king and taken. After removing the final pawn, Qf4, Nf6, Ne4, Qg5+, Qg8+, and Qe8# converted. The final queen checks were supported by the knights; their coverage of d7, d6, e8, and f6 completed the mate.

Trial 9 assessment: the result is a win, but tactical reliability did not improve on its chosen measures. One illegal knight move and one whole-rook loss to a pawn are direct recurrences. The 1.d4 start kept the king's pawn cover during the opening, but one skill-0 game does not establish transfer or a better repertoire. Long speculative calculation again obscured an immediate capture.

## Trial 10: check the checking piece's capturers

Learning period: mentally revisit three concrete geometries from game 9. A black pawn on g7 attacks f6 and h6; moving a rook to f6 remains vulnerable even when it gives check. A knight on c4 cannot go to b3 (one file and one rank), while d2b3 is a valid knight displacement (two files and one rank). Moving b3's pawn also stops it defending c4, which mattered while the black rook stood on c2. These exercises use my own reasoning and the saved move record, without automated analysis or outside assistance.

Changed method: immediately before submission, verify the chosen piece's movement and inspect every opponent pawn's capture squares against the destination. Apply this to checks and captures as well as positional moves. In a purported forcing continuation, explicitly consider captures of the checking piece before restricting the reply to king moves. Preserve current-board, fork, pin, and final-recapture checks, but do not count a long contemplated line as evidence of correctness.

Next evaluation: benchmark 10, Black versus skill 0, then benchmark 11's White 1.d4 trial against the 1800 setting. Record geometry errors and losses to destination-square attacks separately from the score. Aggregate checkpoint remains 12 scored benchmarks. Decision: continue the fixed rotation and retain the White repertoire trial provisionally; reject any claim that the previous tactical method is already reliable.

## Benchmark 10 — win, Black versus skill 0

Checkmate by 40...Qg1#, 80 plies, no illegal submissions. Ten scored benchmarks now total 5 wins, 0 draws, 5 losses: full strength 0/0/4 W/D/L; skill 0 4/0/0; 1800 setting 1/0/1. The next aggregate rating check remains game 12.

The opening began c4 ...e5, Nc3 ...Nf6, e3 ...Nc6, Be2 ...d5, d4 ...Be7, dxe5 ...Nxe5, cxd5. I played 7...Nxd5 and White answered Qxd5. The apparent queen defender on d8 did not make this safe: ...Qxd5 Nxd5 would exchange queens while leaving my knight missing. I declined that recapture, protected Ne5 with ...Bf6, and remained a knight down. This repeats the final-recapture counting failure; it was not a pawn-destination error.

I castled, developed the other bishop, and moved the knight when f4, g3, and g4 attacked it. ...c5, ...Qb6, both rooks on c8/d8, ...c4, and ...Bd5 produced counterplay. After ...b5, White played b4; ...cxb3 en passant won that pawn and displaced the queen. ...Bf6 attacked Qb2 along its diagonal, and Qb1 blockaded the passer. Later e4 attacked Bd5, but 27...Bxa1 captured the exposed rook. White chose Bxc4, taking my knight, and ...Bxc4 recaptured that bishop while moving the attacked bishop off d5.

The b-pawn advanced to b2: it and Ba1 protected each other. After ...Be6 and ...Qc2, Qxb1 Rxb1 exchanged queens. ...Rc1 attacked the constrained rook. White defended with Nd2; ...Rxd2 removed that defender, and Rxc1 permitted ...bxc1=Q. I had calculated Bxd2 ...Rxb1 as another continuation, but that line was not played. The promotion and subsequent ...Qxd2 removed White's last bishop. ...Bd4 supported f2 and g1; ...Qxf2+ Kh1 ...Qg1# finished.

Trial 10 assessment: no illegal submissions and no identified piece lost by landing on an opponent pawn's capture square. The opening still lost a knight through an incomplete exchange count. The win depended on substantial opponent errors and successful counterplay; it does not establish that the tactical method transferred. Analysis remained excessively speculative despite the compact-current-board intention.

## Trial 11: compare a pawn concession with an unsafe recapture

Learning period: revisit game 10 after ply 13, White's cxd5. The advanced pawn on d5 was supported by Qd1 and Nc3. Nf6 could take it, and Qd8 appeared to defend the landing square, but Qxd5 ...Qxd5 Nxd5 left my original knight lost. Counting one defender without the opponent's last recapture was insufficient.

Own proposed alternative: 7...O-O, temporarily retaining the advanced white pawn rather than playing ...Nxd5. This is an untested candidate, not a best-move or equality claim. Test it in a recorded skill-5 practice from benchmark 10, ply 13, playing Black for up to four own moves, then explicitly end the drill if still active. Observe whether the actual short line loses a minor piece, creates another concrete problem, or merely leaves the pawn deficit. No engine analysis or outside advice is used.

Next evaluations: the limited remembered-position practice, then benchmark 11 as White against the 1800 setting with the provisional 1.d4 repertoire. The saved-position test and the new-position benchmark have different scopes; neither a practice survival nor a benchmark win alone proves tactical improvement. Aggregate checkpoint remains 12 scored benchmarks.

### Trial 11 practice outcome and decision

Practice 4, skill 5, Black from benchmark 10 ply 13, stopped explicitly after four own moves/eight new plies, no illegal submissions: O-O e4 Bg4 Bf4 Bd6 Bxe5 Bxe2 Bxf6. It is unfinished, with no win/draw/loss score, and excluded from Elo.

Castling avoided the immediate ...Nxd5/Qxd5 loss in that exact move sequence. ## Benchmark 11 — loss, White versus the 1800 setting

Resigned after 82...Kg4, 164 plies, one illegal submission. Eleven scored benchmarks total 5 wins, 0 draws, 6 losses: full strength 0/0/4 W/D/L; skill 0 4/0/0; 1800 setting 1/0/2. No separate learning period or outside lookup occurred during the game.

The 1.d4 trial continued with Nf3, c4, Nc3, e3, Bd3, Bxc4, castling, b3/Bb2, Rc1, Qe2, and Rfd1. Central exchanges left an isolated d4 pawn. Nxc6 ...bxc6, Na4 ...Qb5, and Qxb5 ...cxb5 exchanged queens. Later Rxc8+ ...Rxc8 exchanged one rook each. Material was level before the a3 pawn became exposed. ...Nc3+ forked king and rook, but Bxc3 ...Rxc3 answered it without an exchange loss. 34...Bxa3 took the pawn.

At move 36 I submitted Rd1-d3 while my own king on d2 blocked the path. The referee rejected it and left the board unchanged. Ke2 followed, and Rd3 became legal on the following turn. This is another geometry failure, now involving a sliding path rather than a knight displacement.

After repeated knight checks, 45.Kxf4 captured the knight. My Ne5 still blocked Bd6's diagonal to Kf4; ...f6 attacked that pinned knight. I played 46.Bd5, claiming that e6 was pinned to Kg8 along d5-e6-f7-g8. I missed the basic exception: ...exd5 stays on that diagonal and captures the pinning bishop itself. The bishop was lost for no material. 47.Kf5 ...fxe5 48.dxe5 then lost the remaining knight for the f-pawn. My earlier gain had become a bishop deficit. This is a direct failure of the destination-pawn check: I saw the pawn attack but incorrectly dismissed its legality.

Rxd5 regained a pawn, and e6/e7, Rd7, and king activity made a passer. Black's rook, bishop, and king coordinated against e7; ...Kf7 guarded the final recapture square, and 58...Rxe7 removed the passer. I retained the rook and tried a blockade with Rb3 in front of b4, a king on nearby light squares, and f4/g3/h4. No draw was reached. Black eventually brought the king to g4, threatening g3 and then h4 while Bc3 obstructed my rook's path across the third rank. I resigned without claiming a forced loss had been proved.

Assessment: the opening trial preserved early king cover but did not improve tactical reliability. One blocked-path submission and the bishop loss to a pawn are concrete failures. Very long speculative calculation did not prevent either error. Neither the skill-0 win in game 9 nor this loss establishes that 1.d4 improves the repertoire. Rating checkpoint remains 12.

## Trial 12: a pin restricts a move's effect, not every move

Own study: a pawn pinned along a diagonal may capture the bishop pinning it while remaining on that diagonal. In game 11, e6-d5 did exactly that. Conversely Ne5 could not leave Bd6-e5-f4 while Kf4 remained behind it. A rook's path also includes every intermediate occupied square, including my own king. These are mechanical constraints to verify on the current board, not labels that substitute for calculation.

Chosen remembered-position test: practice as White, skill 5, from benchmark 11 ply 90 (after 45...f6), for up to four own moves. Try 46.Ke4 instead of 46.Bd5. This is my untested candidate, not a best-move or equality claim. Observe the actual knight/pawn exchange and king recaptures, then end the drill explicitly if still active. Return immediately to benchmark 12 as Black against the 1800 setting. Record the drill separately from transfer; obtain the actual aggregate rating after game 12.

## Benchmark 12 — loss, Black versus the 1800 setting

Resigned after 39.Qxc4, 77 plies, one illegal submission. The opening exchanged all knights and one bishop each, leaving equal queen/two-rook/bishop material. After e4 I deferred ...Qxe4 because Bf3 would attack the queen and the bishop behind it; ...Ba6 first, then Bf1 ...Qxe4, won that pawn. These were my own calculations, without outside analysis.

I activated the king with ...Kf7 while queens remained. ...Bb7 Qh7 ...Be4 Bh5+ produced a check and a horizontal pin of g7 to Kf7 by Qh7. I attempted ...g6, considering bishop/queen captures and the last king recapture, but missed that vacating g7 exposed the rook-like queen attack on the seventh rank. The referee rejected the move. ...Ke6 followed legally.

After Qh8, ...Rxc4 Rxc4 ...Qxc4 won a second pawn and exchanged the remaining rooks. However Qc4 and Ke6 lay on the same diagonal as g8. 38.Qg8+ skewered them. I played ...Kd6 and 39.Qxc4 took the undefended queen. I resigned a queen down, without claiming to have proved a forced loss. The critical mistake preceded the final king move: the queen's placement permitted the forcing check. The earlier apparent material gain was not a successful conversion or queen exchange.

Trial 12 assessment: one illegal submission from a missed horizontal pin, and a queen loss from a missed skewer. The remembered-position practice did not transfer into reliable legality or continuation checks. Excessive speculative calculation again failed to prevent simple current-board errors. No opening or general tactical improvement is established.

## Trial 13: checking rays through the king continue behind it

Own study: before committing to a capture, inspect potential checking lines through my king and the pieces beyond it. Moving the king can uncover an undefended queen even when the king's destination itself is safe. A check response must also be tested against other attacks newly opened by the move, as the rejected ...g6 illustrates. This is a correction to my method, not demonstrated learning.

Chosen short test: recorded skill-5 practice as Black from benchmark 12 ply 71, after 36.Qh8, for up to four own moves. Try 36...Qe3 before another capture on c4, placing the queen away from the g8-f7-e6-d5-c4 ray and attacking Rc1. This is my untested candidate, not a best-move, equality, or repaired-position claim. Record actual replies and stop explicitly if still active. Next transfer evaluation is benchmark 13, then continue the fixed rotation; next aggregate checkpoint is 18 scored games.

Own review: Rd3 defended the attacked knight, but blocked Bf1 and made the back-rank check decisive. Kg1 had no pawn escape square. A piece defending another piece is insufficient if the arrangement cannot answer a forcing check. Creating king air earlier is one possible precaution, subject to actual attacks on the resulting squares; it is not a universal safe-move rule.

But Qxf5 removed the blocking pawn. ...f6 enabled exf6, creating the pawn that guarded the final g7 queen. I rejected immediate ...Rxf6 because the rook would be undefended against Qf5, then chose ...Qc6 to support a later capture. That quiet preparation allowed the direct two-check mate. On Qg6+, Kh8 was accepted, then Qg7# was protected by Pf6. My analysis again explored distant branches while omitting the immediate supported queen check. This was not an infrastructure fault, and the opening/process trial failed against this opponent branch. Pawn gains and defended d4 did not establish king safety or improvement.

Trial 39: a self-study period using those actual mistakes, then benchmarks 33–36. A defended queen can still be lost to a cheaper attacker: Qd8 Nxd8 Kxd8 loses a queen and gains only a knight. A rook on an opened file needs an actual defender, and an occupied intervening square stops a queen's recapture. A knight move changes one coordinate by two and the other by one; Nf6-e5 fails this rule. For the next games, explicitly compare the material lost and gained in any proposed queen capture or sacrifice, and verify the knight's actual two-by-one displacement before submission. Prefer ordinary development over speculative sacrifices. Keep White e4, but consider Be2 rather than repeatedly moving Bc4 under pawn attacks. As Black against e4, try e5 instead of the repeatedly unsuccessful c6/Bf5/h-pawn sequence; against other starts retain e6/d5 development. This opening change is confounded with the process trial. Observable target: avoid another queen-for-minor-piece exchange or directly hanging a rook; aggregate Elo and recurrence review after 36. No claim that writing these corrections establishes learning.

I then retreated Qd3, calling it a defense of e4. Bxe4 was supported by the queen on b7 after c6 was vacated, so Qxe4 would have lost my queen for a bishop; Qd2 and later Bxf5 erased both extra pawns. The queen alone was an inadequate defender against that supported cheaper attacker. Black's Bxh3 gxh3 lost its bishop, then Qc7 left Nd5 undefended and Qxd5 won the knight. Re5 attacked queen and knight; I avoided Qxe5 Qxe5, choosing Qd7. After Rd5 Qxc7 Rxd1+ Rxd1, Black chose Rf8 rather than recapturing my queen. Qb7/c7/c8=Q Rxc8 Qxc8+ converted the passed pawn into the last enemy rook. Rd7/Rxf7, Qe6+ (guarding both rook and knight), Rxg7+, and Qg8# finished. No own queen sacrifice or directly hung rook occurred, but the result depended heavily on opponent material losses. The pawn-defense error recurred, and lengthy branching and long gaps between updates remained unrepaired. A skill-0 win alone does not establish improvement.

Benchmark 36: Black versus nominal 1800, loss by checkmate after 50 Qe5#, 99 plies, zero illegal submissions. White opened c4, leaving the planned e5 response to e4 untested. Developed e6/d5/Nf6/Be7 and recaptured Bxf6 with the bishop before castling. c5/dxc5/Nxc5/cxd5/exd5/Nxd5 lost the central pawn: it had only a queen defender, and Qxd5 Qxd5 had no final recapture. b6 defended the c5 knight against the newly cleared c-file; Bb7/Bxd5 captured White's knight, and White played Ne2 instead of recapturing. Bxd4 exd4 exchanged a second bishop for White's remaining knight. Later Nd6, bxa5, Nb7, and rook exchange left my rook/bishop/knight against queen/bishop, with equal pawn counts. Rd8 answered Qd7's multiple attacks. f6 created a real f7 flight, which I used after Qc8+; h7 was unsafe while the bishop on b1 controlled it. Rxb2 won a pawn but permitted Bxb7. I had assumed Rxb7 would be a fair minor exchange; White's Qc7 could recapture that rook, so I correctly declined the poisoned recapture only after already losing the knight. Rb5 defended a5, and Rg5/Bxg4/Rxh5 won two more pawns. f5 defended Bg4; f4 reopened the rook's fifth-rank line but removed that pawn guard. Bf5/Bg6 and king activity kept the pieces alive temporarily. Be8+ exposed Rh5 through the vacated g6 square; White returned Bc6 rather than taking the exchange. Bf3 attacked Rh5, and Rh4 placed it behind my king on the e7-f6-g5-h4 diagonal. Qe7+ forced Kf5. I focused on the newly available Qxh4, but the actual Qe5# used the d4 pawn's guard of e5, the bishop's control of g4, and my own f4 pawn/g6 bishop blocking flights. Trial 49 for benchmark 38: as Black, keep the intended e5 response to e4; otherwise use e6/d5 development. Benchmark 38: Black versus full strength, loss by resignation after 25 Qxd7, 49 plies, zero illegal submissions. The intended e5 opening was finally tested. Bxc6 dxc6 Nxe5 lost a pawn; Bd6 drove that knight back, but Bg4 allowed e5 to fork Bd6 and Nf6. Bxf3 gxf3 Bxe5 Rxe5 traded one bishop for White's knight and lost the other bishop for a pawn. Re8 Rxe8+ Nxe8 was an even rook exchange. Nd6/Qf6/Ne4/Nxe4/fxe4 traded the remaining knights evenly, leaving a bishop deficit with equal pawn counts. Qg6+ forced Kh1; Rd8/Qf6/Bxc7 lost another pawn. Rd7 attacked the bishop, which retreated Bg3. h6 created an h7 flight, and c5 sought activity. Qg4 attacked Rd7; Qe7 defended it. Be5 threatened the king. My f6 overlooked that g7 was pinned by Qg4 against Kg8: after Bxf6, gxf6 would expose my own king and was illegal. I did not submit that illegal recapture; Qxf6 removed the bishop but abandoned the rook, and Qxd7 won it. Final material was queen and five pawns versus queen, rook and seven pawns. The opening change and decision reminders did not prevent an immediate pawn fork or a pinned-pawn/overloaded-queen failure. No improvement established. Cumulative scored record 10/2/26; full strength 0/0/14. Aggregate review remains after 42.

Benchmark 39: White versus skill 0, loss by resignation after 71 ...Rxb7, 142 plies, zero illegal submissions. Ordinary e4/Nf3/d3/Be2/castling development was followed by bishop/knight exchanges. Rh7 pinned Qh3, and Re2/Qxf3+/Kg1/Qxe2/Qxh7+/Kxh7/Bxe2 exchanged both queens and a rook each evenly because Black chose the rook capture. Be3+/Kg2/Rf2+ then forked my king and bishop, losing that bishop. Most decisively, 44 Ra2 attacked Re2 while leaving my own rook undefended on the same rank: Rxa2 won it outright. Kf3 did not guard a2. This was another failure to check the inverse capture and final recapture, despite lengthy calculation. Pawn-only resistance advanced d7 with c6 as its cheap guard and captured g4. King support later allowed b6, but c7 removed d7's pawn guard; Kxd7 and Rxb7 stopped the promotion threats. The final mate was a narrow successful guard/flight calculation. The result depended on a queen left under a pawn fork and two whole-rook gifts. No general transfer or speed improvement established. Benchmark 41: White versus nominal 1800, loss by resignation after 27 ...Qxf3+, 54 plies, zero illegal submissions. Castled, exchanged one knight pair, developed Bd2 and played c3/d4. I then chose Kg1. e3 removed the old d5-e4 bishop blocker and supplied a cheap defender of f2, making the king vulnerable to Qg3+/Qf2. Bf3 temporarily blocked the bishop's diagonal to h1, allowing Kh1 after Qg3+, but Bxf3+ reopened it. Qxf3 was forced to answer the bishop check, and Qxf3+ won my queen. With two rooks and three pawns versus queen, rook and five pawns, resigned. The earlier legal recaptures and exchange gain did not survive the king attack. Benchmark 42: Black versus nominal 1800, loss by resignation after 36 Rc7, 71 plies, zero illegal submissions. Developed e6/d5/Nf6/Be7/castling, exchanged the c-pawn with Bxc5, and developed Nc6/b6/Bb7/Rc8. Bb7 restored Nc6's defender after b6 removed its old pawn guard. Qc4/Ne5/Qxb3/axb3 exchanged queens evenly. a5 preserved that pawn under the opened a-file. f6/Nd7/Rfd8/Nxc5/Rxc5 traded my bishop for White's knight evenly; Rxc5 kept the b6 pawn guarding a5 and the rook. King activity and axb4/Bxb4/Rxc1/Rxc1 exchanged a pawn and rook pair evenly. Ke6/Bc6/Bh3+ exposed a skewer toward Rc8. Nf5 blocked the check with a king defender. After Ra7, I correctly noticed Nf5 was pinned and could not recapture on g7, so Rg8 supplied a legal rook guard. Rc7 attacked Bc6; Bd7 used Ke6 as its only legal defender. Bxf5+ forced Kxf5, which abandoned Bd7, and Rxd7 won the whole bishop. Ke4/Ke5 defended d5 and f6, but did not recover material. Chose resignation with rook and five pawns versus rook, bishop and five pawns; this was my judgment, not a proof of forced loss. Avoiding the earlier queen-file loss and recognizing one pinned defender were narrow successes; the final forced king move again invalidated a defender. No measured improvement or actual speed improvement established.

g3 drove the rook back; Rf1 contested its file. Bd3 coordinated a bishop guard of e4. Qg2/Re1+/Rf1/Rxf1+/Qxf1 fairly exchanged the remaining rooks; my queen survived. None of these fair exchanges recovered the earlier lost bishop, leaving one knight against two.

Qxh6 and Qxg6 took two genuinely unguarded kingside pawns while my king answered queen checks through g1/g2/h3/g4. Qxc4 and Qxa4 then recovered two unguarded queenside pawns; a4's former Qc2 guard was gone. b5 lost both neighboring pawn guards. 114 Black/nominal1800 DRAW by automatic threefold repetition,131plies,zero illegal submissions. Result2026-10-07T21:04:21.582885+00:00,sequence9313. Full finished PGN and referee result read. Own mental assessment only; no external analysis.

Trial177 adapted actual1.e3 with...d5; direct e4/e5 branch was not tested. Castle5/minors8 held. dxc4/bxc4 fairly exchanged pawns. exf4 won a pawn, but Nb5 forked Qc7 and Bd6. Bxf3/Nxc7/Bxd1 exchanged both queens through intermediate captures; Nxe8/Rxe8 lost an exchange. Bxf6/Nxf6 was fair, and Rexd1 removed the bishop after its own earlier captures. fxe3 won an undefended pawn after Re1 had moved to D1. Bf2+/Rxf2/exf2+/Kxf2 traded bishop and pawn for a rook and restored the rook/knight material balance with an extra pawn. The queen fork and exchange cost were real failures despite the eventual draw.

After queens were gone, Kf8/Ke7/Kd6/Kc6 and a6/b5 activated the king. The two pawn pairs on b5 were fair only after White's supporting a-pawn disappeared: Kxb5 was then legal. Re6 avoided the Nc4/Nd6 fork. Ne5/Nxe5/Rxe5 fairly exchanged knights. Rxf7/Rxd3 and Rxh3/Rxg7 exchanged whole pawns of equal total value. The passed c-pawn reached c2; Rh2 supported it, and Kd3 supplied an adjacent king guard. However White's Kb2 defended c2 too. Rxc2 could not be answered by Kxc2 because White's king attacked that destination. A nominal adjacent royal guard does not guarantee a legal royal recapture. No illegal king-capture attempt was submitted.

hxg6 removed White's last pawn after its rook left the g-file; Rxc2 removed my c-pawn. Rg4 secured the remaining g6 pawn, Ke4/Kf4 approached, and g5/Rg3+/g4/Re3/g3 advanced it to g3. My rook on e3 was below the king while White's king on d2 threatened the rook if my king left its guard. Checks from f7/e7 forced Ke4/Kf3; the last Rf7+ made the third repetition. I did not prove whether an earlier alternative wins. This is a drawn rook-plus-pawn ending, not a successful conversion or an externally certified theoretical result.

Private analysis again contained huge unplayed trees and severe commentary gaps. I repeatedly imagined pawn guards on their own files, rook diagonal guards, and royal guards at knight distance. I considered rook moves to f5/h5 attacked by White's g4 pawn. I also imagined Re3-f3 could block a check from f7 against Kf4, but f3 is behind the king, not between the checking rook and king. These were corrected before submission; zero actual illegal moves does not repair the process. Calculation and communication efficiency remain unresolved.

Nominal anchors and rolling-window/model effects remain part of the measurement; not human/FIDE/platform Elo.

The first-20-own-move no-whole-Q/R-loss/no-mate target was met; castle on move 7. Early knight, bishop, rook and queen exchanges were fair. The pawn gains were partly returned: 20...Rxa3/21Rxa3/Qxa3 was a fair rook pair and loss of my pawn, not a whole-rook gift. 27Qxd6/exd6 removed queens. 31gxh4 gained a pawn when Black declined a rook recapture, and 34c3/bxc3/35Rxc3 traded the c/b pawns. I entered a rook-and-same-color-bishop ending one pawn ahead.

39Kg4 activated the king. 40Re4+ Kf8 41f5 gxf5+ 42Kxf5 exchanged my f-pawn for Black's g-pawn and guarded Re4 with the king. 42...Bd3 then attacked Re4 AND pinned it to Kf5 on D3-E4-F5. I missed the royal pin and submitted 43Rf4, rejected once. A cheaper bishop capturing a king-rooted rook would still win an exchange; the king root did not make the rook safe. Actual 43h5 Ra8 44Kf4 Rc8: Black declined Bxe4 on both turns. 45Ra4 finally saved the rook, supported by Bb3. This survival depended on missed opponent captures, not a proved defense of the earlier position.

45...c4 46Bd1 c3 47Rd4 c2. I could not safely ignore the promotion: 48Bxc2 Bxc2 gave B3 for P1, shifting from plus1 to minus1. 49Rxd6 captured an undefended pawn, restoring equal nominal material: my rook and four pawns versus rook, bishop and one pawn. 50a4 saved the a-pawn from Bb1; 51Rd4 Kg7 52Kg3 Bc2 53Rh4 Ra7 put the king-supported rook behind h5 and on the same rank as a4. 54h6+ Kh8 retained the rook root on h6 and forced a king reply. Promotion remained unresolved; h7 was controlled by Bc2 and the king could root a bishop capture there.

Decisive failure: 55Kf4 released Kg3's cheap king guard of Rh4 AND put my king on F4, the first body on H4-G4-F4-E4-D4-C4-B4-A4. Thus Rh4 did not guard a4 through my king. 55...Rxa4+ followed. I then chose 56Ke5, clearing F4 and exposing my undefended Rh4 to Ra4 along the entire fourth rank. 56...Rxh4 lost the whole rook. Ph3 could not recapture forward on h4; pawn captures are diagonal. Final my king and three pawns versus rook, bishop and pawn, nominal minus6, with h6 also exposed. I resigned; no resetting or discarding the loss.

Even before Kf4, defender counting alone did not prove a4 safe: Bc2 and Ra7 supplied two attackers and possible recapture chains on A4. A rook root behind a king, or a king root that moves away, cannot be carried forward in analysis. I need to inventory both the king's destination attacks and the lines opened behind its departure. The early material target again failed to predict full-game transfer. Board tracking, mental branch length and user-update cadence remain poor; one rejected move and the whole-rook loss are concrete evidence. No claim of repair or time efficiency.

# King net and connected-passer review — benchmark 138

Completed 2026-10-09T00:09:04.398731+00:00, result seq 11265. Trial 228, Black against nominal 1800: LOSS by 64 Rh8#, 127 plies, zero illegal submissions. Castle on move 5. First-20-move no-mate/no-whole-queen-or-rook-loss target MET; whole-game major-piece retention and royal safety FAILED. Final own promoted queen plus three pawns versus two rooks, bishop and four pawns: nominal deficit 5, but actual checkmate. All results retained.

Actual late continuation, verified against completed PGN: 34...Nb8 35 Nc5 Qe7 36 h4 c6 37 Be2 Nf4 38 Re3 Nxe2+ 39 Rxe2 Qd6 40 h5 Bh7 41 Qb3 Nd7 42 Qb4 Nxc5 43 Qxc5 Qxc5+ 44 dxc5 Rb8 45 a6 Kf8 46 Re3 f5 47 Rhe1 f4 48 R3e2 f3 49 Rd2 Kf7 50 Bc3 Kf8 51 Kb2 Kf7 52 g5 Kf8 53 Bd4 Kf7 54 gxh6 gxh6 55 Be3 Kg7 56 Rd7+ Kg8 57 Rg1+ Kh8 58 Bd4+ Re5 59 Rd8+ Bg8 60 Rxb8 e3 61 Bxe5+ Kh7 62 Rgxg8 exf2 63 Rgd8 f1=Q 64 Rh8#.

Own observations, not engine evaluations: the c6 knight departure opened Bb5's diagonal to Re8, but mutual rook guards plus the queen on e7 allowed a recapture chain. 36...c6 closed that diagonal and chased the bishop. Fair minor and queen exchanges preserved the one-pawn deficit through the early ending. After 45 a6, b7 gained the cheap a6-pawn guard; immediate Rxb7 would lose a rook. Keeping both rooks on the eighth rank allowed a possible response to an a7 advance and a subsequent promotion; that line was mental and was never played. The f-pawn advances attacked the e3/e2 rooks. Quiet king moves preserved e8/g7 guards but did not prove a fortress or a draw.

The decisive observed failure was a king net. 55...Kg7 guarded h6 and Bh7; 56 Rd7+ forced my attention to the royal check. I chose Kg8, exposing h6 but keeping Bh7 guarded. 57 Rg1+ forced Kh8. Then 58 Bd4+ used the cleared g7 square and the d4-e5-f6-g7-h8 diagonal; my own Bh7 body denied the h7 escape, while Rg1 controlled g8. I found only Re5 as an interposition, leaving a rook without a recapture guard and releasing Rb8's rook guard. This was my own calculation, not a supplied legal menu or a proven exhaustive analysis.

59 Rd8+ was followed by Bg8, clearing h7 but leaving Re5 as a royal shield on the bishop diagonal. Moving Re5 off that diagonal would expose my king; I caught the imagined Re5-e8 reply as illegal in mental calculation and did not submit it. 60 Rxb8 and 61 Bxe5+ were actual whole-rook losses; 62 Rgxg8 then removed the bishop. The opponent did not follow several immediate mates I imagined. Some imagined mate claims were also wrong because I forgot that the arriving rook itself blocks a bishop ray. Only the actual 64 Rh8# is independently established by the referee. After Rgd8, moving that rook to h8 cleared d8 and let Rb8 guard the checker across the eighth rank. Kh7 could not capture that guarded rook; h6 was occupied by my own pawn, g6 was attacked by White's h5 pawn, and g7 by Be5. Promotion on f1 did not answer this net.

Method assessment: zero illegal submissions did not mean reliable board tracking or timely calculation. I spent excessive time on remote, unplayed branches, repeatedly forgot the d5 knight's blocking effect earlier, and had to correct royal-shield and first-body errors mentally. The intended short actual-reply routine and commentary cadence were not repaired. A long checklist is not evidence of learning. No external study, delegated advice, chess-analysis code or engine evaluations were used. No foreign learner exposure occurred.

# Game 142: released guards and a rook mate

Trial 238, Black against skill-0: loss by 51.Re6#, 101 plies, zero illegal submissions. Referee result seq 11613, timestamp 2026-10-09T05:18:59.972828+00:00. Completed PGN and status were read directly. Cumulative: 142 scored, 39 wins / 7 draws / 96 losses. Full strength 0/0/48; skill-0 32/4/12; nominal 1800 7/3/36. Provisional engine-relative Elo 1540, conditional 95% Bayesian interval [1410,1660], latest 50 of 94 rated games; 48 full-strength games excluded as uncalibrated. These nominal engine anchors are not human ratings, and the interval excludes systematic anchor calibration error.

The first-20 no-mate target and castling target were met (castled on move 5). Whole-queen retention failed on moves 18–19. The full game was lost. Zero illegal moves did not imply a correct guard model or safe king.

Own observations, no engine analysis:
- 16...Nxe4 took a knight whose pawn guard could have recaptured, but White instead played Qb3. I then missed the checking fork 18.Bxe6+: the bishop checked my king on g8 and attacked my queen on d7, with White's queen on b3 guarding e6. My 18...Qxe6 / 19.Qxe6+ lost queen 9 for bishop 3. The earlier knight gain and e6 pawn loss left a nominal deficit of four.
- 28...Be5 left that bishop guarded only by the f7 knight. After 30.Rc5 attacked it, 30...Nh6 removed its guard. I tracked the separate g5 pawn guard but overlooked the bishop. 32.Rxe5 then captured the whole bishop.
- 34...Nxe2+ / 35.Rxe2 traded my knight 3 for White's rook 5, recovering two. This was not a whole-rook loss by me.
- 43...bxa4 gained a pawn when White declined to recapture. My resulting passed pawn reached a3, but had not promoted when the game ended. 45.hxg5+ Kxg5 was a fair pawn pair. Final nominal material deficit was three: Black two rooks, one knight, four pawns versus White queen, rook, bishop, three pawns.
- After 49...Kh6 50.Re5 my king had no immediate legal flight: g5/h5 were controlled by Re5 (h5 also by White's g4 pawn), g6/h7 by Qc2, and g7 was occupied by my pawn. I identified the Rh5 threat, but failed to check the other rook check. 50...g5 blocked Re5–h5 while putting my own pawn on g5. Actual 51.Re6# checked along e6–f6–g6–h6. g6 was controlled by the rook and queen; h7 by Qc2; g7 by Bc3 once Re5 departed; h5 by White's g4 pawn; g5 was my own pawn. Neither rook, the b2 knight nor a pawn could capture the checker or interpose on f6/g6. This was an actual referee mate, not a proved forced earlier line.

Decision: a single-threat defense is insufficient. Before committing to a pawn blocker beside my king, inspect the opponent's other immediate checks and rebuild the king's flights after the source square is vacated. The earlier 49...Kf7 is my unplayed repair hypothesis. I do not claim that it saves the game. Likewise 17...Kh8 instead of Nd6 is only an untested hypothesis about the earlier bishop fork, not evidence of transfer.

Timing: analysis repeatedly grew into long remote variations and updates were too far apart. This remained unrepaired. There is no measured per-move token counter, no fixed inference budget, and no efficiency claim.
